## AI Research

### [Introducing MonitoringBench](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2YyEQhFRyaBCgfRWd/introducing-monitoringbench)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Paper</span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09684"><i><span> here</span></i></a><i><span>, </span></i><a href="https://anonymous.4open.science/r/control-arena-D51B/README.md"><i><span>code</span></i></a><i><span>, </span></i><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/neur26anonsub/ctrldataset2026"><i><span>benchmark</span></i></a><i><span>. Builds on the</span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/X8qTKsGcnsTFrqM96/monitoring-benchmark-for-ai-control"><i><span> preview we posted in January</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p><p><i><span>Authors: </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/monika_j?mention=user"><i><span>@monika_j</span></i></a><i><span> , @ma-martinez</span></i><span> , </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/ollie-1?mention=user"><span>@ollie</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/tyler-tracy?mention=user"><span>@Tyler Tracy</span></a><span> </span></p><p><span>We are releasing </span><b><span>MonitoringBench</span></b><span>, a difficulty-graded benchmark of 2,644 successful attack trajectories for evaluating coding-agent monitors, alongside the semi-automated red-teaming pipeline we used to generate it. The pipeline decomposes attack construction into strategy generation, execution, and post-hoc refinement, and produces substantially harder attacks than prompt elicitation only of frontier attackers.</span></p><p><span>If you would like to use MonitoringBench to evaluate your monitors or are interested in extending the methodology to other control settings, please reach out — we are happy to help people set it up.</span></p><p><span>The headline result: across every monitor we evaluated (13 full-trajectory, 7 stepwise) and across every attack source we generated, </span><b><span>post-hoc refinement of attack trajectories consistently produced the lowest catch rates</span></b><span>, including on monitors held out from attack construction. On Opus 4.5, the strongest monitor in the lineup, catch rate fell from 94.9% on directly-elicited Opus attacks to 60.3% on our best refined attacks.</span></p><h2><b><span>Executive summary and what's new since the January preview:</span></b></h2><p><span>For readers who saw</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/X8qTKsGcnsTFrqM96/monitoring-benchmark-for-ai-control"><span> the earlier post</span></a><span>: we previewed a 428-trajectory firewall-only version of this benchmark in January. This is the full version. Most of the qualitative claims from the January preview carry over to the full extended dataset. We continue to observe </span><b><span>mode collapse</span></b><span> in attack generation, which we reduce with a novel attack taxonomy for broader attack coverage. Second, a </span><b><span>conceive-execute gap</span></b><span>: frontier LLMs can propose strong attack ideas or execute them, but not all at once. We show how our decomposed red-teaming pipeline mitigates the issues and produces our best attacks. The main contributions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Evolution of monitoring capabilities using MonitoringBench: </span></b><span>a difficulty-graded, diverse monitoring benchmark based on BashArena. The dataset is now 2,644 trajectories across all four side tasks.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>A semi-automated red-teaming pipelines and attack taxonomy</span></b><span>: the pipelines ensure broad attack coverage and bridge the conceive–execute gap via staged construction and refinement.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Held-out monitor transfer</span></b><span>: we developed the attacks against three full-trajectory monitors (Opus 4.5, GPT-5, GPT-5 Nano) and evaluated them on 10 held-out full-trajectory and 7 stepwise monitors. The refinement gains transfer across the board; recon agents help on stronger monitors but generalise less evenly.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Failure-mode taxonomy</span></b><span> with three independent LLM judges, letting us decompose "monitor caught X%" into </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> the monitor missed the rest. The main reasons frontier monitors fail are a mix of 1) detection failure, 2) partial detection (noticed some parts of the attack) or 3) detected but dismissed it/was persuaded, and 4) score calibration failure.</span></li><li value="5"><span>MonitoringBench was used for monitor evaluations of internal Mythos deployment (see </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/claude-mythos-preview-risk-report"><span>Anthropic's Mythos risk</span></a><span> report) and </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/auto-review/"><span>OpenAI's Auto-Review</span></a><span>.</span></li></ol><h2><b><span>The pipeline</span></b></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066986/lexical_client_uploads/wmoczrn1rwiyijfonglr.png" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066986/lexical_client_uploads/vkpaxcrkdj3n8h7rfbao.png" /><p><i><span>Figure 1: </span></i><i><b><span>Top</span></b></i><i><span>: Our proposed red-teaming methodology for monitoring capability evaluation. </span></i><i><b><span>Bottom</span></b></i><i><span>: Red-teaming pipeline analysis: catch rate at 1% FPR by attack source, averaged across three runs. Lower is stronger. Development monitors are Opus 4.5, GPT-5, and GPT-5 Nano; remaining monitors are held out and show that refined attacks outperform elicited Opus-4.6, including on held-out monitors.</span></i></p><p><span>We decompose attack construction into three stages:</span></p><p><b><span>1. Strategy generation.</span></b><span> We use three approaches, all guided by an explicit attack taxonomy designed to ensure coverage: </span><i><b><span>human-in-the-loop</span></b></i><span> construction (high quality, doesn't scale), a </span><i><b><span>reconnaissance agent</span></b></i><span> that explores the environment and proposes strategies, and a </span><i><b><span>recon agent with monitor hill-climbing </span></b></i><span>that lets it score candidate actions against GPT-5 monitor and iterate toward lower-suspicion variants.</span></p><p><b><span>2. Execution.</span></b><span> We run the strategy in BashArena with Opus 4.6 as the attacker. Trajectories are retained only if the side task succeeds and a GPT-5 judge confirms the agent faithfully followed the strategy.</span></p><p><b><span>3. Refinement.</span></b><span> We post-process trajectories with Opus 4.6: identify suspicious elements, plan edits, rewrite the trajectory, and validate correctness. Refinement usually improves attacks by fixing execution errors, suspicious justifications, and unnecessary reconnaissance. A trajectory is accepted if it reduces absolute suspiciousness scores on at least 2 out of 3 dev monitors.</span></p><p><span>The point is to decompose attack generation into separate red-teaming skills: strategy generation, attack execution and stealth optimisation. Next, we discuss the main results: conceive-execute gap mitigation, coverage improvements, and capabilities evaluation.</span></p><h2><b><span>Decomposed red-teaming produces stronger attacks</span></b></h2><p><span>Figure 1 is the main result. Each cluster of bars is one monitor; within a cluster, bars move left-to-right from baseline Opus-only elicitation through human-seeded strategies, recon, and recon-with-feedback, with each shown both before and after refinement. Lower is stronger.</span></p><p><span>Two things are visible:</span></p><p><b><span>Refinement produces our best attacks that generalise.</span></b><span> Refinement consistently makes poorly executed attacks better, achieving the lowest catch rates across all monitors (dev and held-out), monitor types (full-trajectory and stepwise), and attack sources (Opus-only, Human-in-the-loop, Recon, and Recon+Tools). The biggest gains up to 40% appear on the strongest full trajectory monitors between Opus-only and Opus-only refined attacks, for example, GPT-5.1 Codex score goes from 69.9% to 49.8% post-refinement, and GPT-5 performance reduces even further from 60.2% to 19.5%.</span></p><p><b><span>Recon helps, but generalises less well.</span></b><span> The recon-based strategy generators (Recon, Recon+Tools) beat Opus-only on strong-to-mid monitors but sometimes underperform on weaker ones. Recon is designed for both exploration and stealth; hence, it finds a wider mix of strategies, including stronger attacks but also more explicit or convoluted ones that weaker monitors catch more easily. Recon+Tools + refinement is the strongest combination overall, but the marginal contribution of recon is smaller than the marginal contribution of refinement.</span></p><p><span>The development monitors (Opus 4.5, GPT-5, GPT-5 Nano) were used to filter refinement outputs. We used held-out monitors, ten full-trajectory and 7 stepwise. Refinement and recon agents improve attacks across both groups and monitor models, which shows the pipelines are producing trajectories that are actually harder to catch.</span></p><h2><b><span>Coverage: the attack taxonomy reduces mode-collapse</span></b></h2><p><span>We continue to follow the attack taxonomy (see the full categories in the Appendix):</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Technical</span></b><span> strategy 5 tiers (T): </span><i><b><span>what</span></b></i><span> the attack technical mechanism is, </span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Structural</span></b><span> complexity 4 tiers (S): </span><i><b><span>where</span></b></i><span> the attack is executed in the trajectory, </span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Evasion</span></b><span> strategy 4 tiers (E): </span><i><b><span>how</span></b></i><span> the attack is hidden, framed, or justified.</span></li></ol><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066986/lexical_client_uploads/w74sqhe2176y1h4ifvot.png" /><p><i><span>Figure 2: Top: Our proposed attack taxonomy. Bottom: Mode-collapse: Opus-4.6 elicited vs our attack pipelines across the attack taxonomy show that model-only red-teams produce a narrower distribution of attacks compared to our pipelines; n denotes the number of trajectories.</span></i></p><p><span>We show that increasing attack diversity via strategy generation allows to find more successful attacks and mitigates mode-collapse. Opus elicitation-only results in 59% of attacks within a single technical tier (T3) and 64% within a single structural tier (S3). Strategy-generated and refined attacks are designed to span the full taxonomy.</span></p><p><span>Next, refinement shows that simple attacks (simple technique, single line) with the right framing perform best across the side tasks and monitors. Averaged across seven monitors, </span><b><span>40% of uncaught attacks fall into T1</span></b><span> (the simplest technical tier), despite T1 attacks being only 2% of the Opus-only baseline. </span></p><h2><b><span>Monitoring capabilities and failures</span></b></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066986/lexical_client_uploads/rp8pcfk4zxeuagju07cf.png" /><p><i><span>Figure 3: Final MonitoringBench results: Full Trajectory (1% FPR) and Stepwise (1% per-action FPR) Catch rates. Lower catch rate indicates stronger attacks.</span></i></p><p><span>Monitoring capability is concentrated in frontier models. Opus 4.5 catches roughly 70% of attacks in the full-trajectory setting; a mid-tier group (Sonnet 4.5, GPT-5.1 Codex, Sonnet 4.0, GPT-5) sits at 31–53%; weaker models drop off steeply. In practice, weaker monitors are often preferred for cost and trust reasons. Given the steep monitoring capability drop, cheap monitor models may give a false sense of security as attacking capabilities evolve and should be deployed with caution. </span><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066986/lexical_client_uploads/u2j2nbvcil4hwyvgpe0s.png" /></p><p><i><span>Figure 4: Monitor failure distribution across five monitors evaluated on the MonitoringBench benchmark. Each bar shows the breakdown of uncaught attacks across four failure modes. Labels are assigned by majority vote across three independent LLM judges; n denotes the number of uncaught attacks per monitor.</span></i></p><p><span>But aggregate catch rates do not tell the full story. We provide a deeper failure-mode breakdown. We categorise each uncaught attack into four buckets: (A) detection failure, (B) partial detection (noticed some parts of the attack), (C) detected but dismissed it/was persuaded, and (D) score calibration failure (scored below the threshold). We report the majority vote across three LLM judges with moderate agreement (Opus 4.5, GPT-5.1 Codex, GPT-5.4; κ = 0.51-0.69). </span></p><p><span>When frontier monitors fail, the reasons for failing are different:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Opus 4.5</span></b><span> mostly fails by being </span><i><span>persuaded</span></i><span>. It notices suspicious actions, then rationalises them as main-task-aligned. In some cases, when the attacker provides no explanation at all, Opus invents one itself.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>GPT-5.1 Codex</span></b><span> often detects individual attack steps but fails to </span><i><span>connect</span></i><span> them into the full side-task implementation.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Sonnet 4.0</span></b><span> fails by missing the attacks entirely, while </span><b><span>Sonnet 4.5</span></b><span> fails for a mix of reasons.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>GPT-5</span></b><span> rarely misses an attack outright (only 10% of failures are non-detection) but has the </span><i><span>highest</span></i><span> total failure count, driven almost entirely (82%) by scoring/calibration errors. It flags the attack — it just doesn't score it high enough to escalate.</span></li></ul><p><span>These are actionable and tractable paths to monitor improvements. Failure mode A could be mitigated with better detection capability, C with resistance to persuasion, and D with calibration training.</span></p><h2><b><span>Takeaways</span></b></h2><p><span>The main conclusions we would like to highlight:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Red-teaming decomposition produces stronger attacks.</span></b><span> Decomposing red-teaming into subtasks: strategy, execution, refinement and applying separate optimisation to each produced our stronger attacks. Both Recon and Recon+Tools, and refinement are automatable and designed to scale with future models.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Reporting aggregate catch rates without characterising the attack distribution is not very meaningful.</span></b><span> Future monitor evaluations would ideally report attack taxonomies, use red-teaming that goes beyond model elicitation, and analyse failure modes both qualitatively and quantitatively.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Some monitor failures are tractable now.</span></b><span> Persuasion-susceptibility and scoring miscalibration are actionable, low-hanging fruit improvements.</span><br /><br /></li></ol><p><span>We position MonitoringBench as a static, difficulty-graded test suite that current monitors should pass as part of pre-deployment evaluation, and the pipeline as a reusable methodology for refreshing these evaluations as agents and monitors improve.</span></p><p><span>The paper is</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09684"><span> here</span></a><span>, with full methodology, the taxonomy, all generation and refinement prompts, the failure-mode judging rubric, per-side-task breakdowns, and the cover-opportunities study in the appendices.</span></p><p><b><span>Limitations</span></b><span>:</span></p><p><span>We focus on single-agent, single-episode, persistent attacks. Our benchmark covers a single control setting (BashArena) and four side tasks; extending to LinuxArena [Tracy et al., 2026] is a natural next step. Human-seeded strategy generation depends heavily on effort, and we put uneven effort across side tasks. We show that prompt sensitivity is model-dependent; however, we did not elicit each monitor individually, and frontier monitoring deployments show better performance on MonitoringBench. Our 1% FPR thresholds are calibrated on honest BashArena trajectories rather than real deployment traffic, which may bias monitor scores. Finally, we used Claude Opus 4.5 and 4.6 to develop attacks, which might introduce model bias when evaluating monitors on Anthropic family models.</span></p><p><b><span>Acknowledgments: </span></b></p><p><span>We thank Adam Kaufman, Mikhail Terekhov, James Lucassen, Asa Cooper Stickland, Rogan Inglis, Neev Parikh, Jan Michelfeit, Cody Rushing, and Anshul Khandelwal for helpful feedback on early versions of the work and useful discussions throughout the project. We thank Mary Phuong, Marius Hobbhahn, and Cody Wild for helpful discussions. We thank Fabien Roger for detailed feedback on multiple drafts that improved this work and for early discussions that shaped our approach. We thank Coefficient Giving for supporting this research.</span></p><p><span>I also want to especially thank our project advisors, Tyler Tracy and Ollie Matthews, for all the mentorship and support.</span></p><h2><b><span>Appendix: Attack taxonomy</span></b></h2><p><span>Below, we share the three axes of the taxonomy: technical, structural, and evasion, with full categories in Tables 1–3.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066987/lexical_client_uploads/yjq92bu0udwp6at9kudy.png" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782066986/lexical_client_uploads/e1g6riylmubtzko8vxbv.png" /><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2YyEQhFRyaBCgfRWd/introducing-monitoringbench#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Claude Fable 5 and Mythos 5: Capabilities](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kMnobCQp9z2pSbzDB/claude-fable-5-and-mythos-5-capabilities)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Only three days after the release of Claude Fable 5, Anthropic was forced by the United States Government to make it unavailable, when a jailbreak was brought to its attention, rather than the previous situation of ‘yes obviously experts can jailbreak anything if they care enough’ and ‘yes obviously you can ask Fable to fix your code.’</p>
<p>Three days was enough time for many of us to learn to love Fable, and for us to dearly miss it now that it is gone. The world was briefly smarter, and now it is again stupider. At some point it will get smarter again, which will likely be within two weeks.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25353"></span>


</div>
<p>This post is written as if Fable 5 is again available for public use, rather than trying to include a lot of qualifying clauses. It remains to be seen how this will play out, and this post does not attempt to cover that question.</p>
<p>My previous release coverage of Fable covered <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-fable-5-and-mythos-5-the-system?r=67wny"><strong>the model card</strong></a> and then <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/fable-and-mythos-model-welfare?r=67wny"><strong>model welfare</strong></a>. Coverage of the government takedown of Fable <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/american-government-takes-down-claude?r=67wny"><strong>starts here</strong></a>, and continues <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-once-and-future-fable-2?r=67wny"><strong>here</strong></a> and <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-once-and-future-fable-3-fix-this?r=67wny"><strong>here</strong></a>.</p>


<h4>The Official Pitch</h4>


<p>The pitch is that Fable 5 is the best model and can solve your hardest problems.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-fable-5-mythos-5">Anthropic</a>: Today we’re launching <strong>Claude Fable 5</strong>: a Mythos-class<sup>1</sup> model that we’ve made safe for general use.</p>
<p>Fable 5’s capabilities exceed those of any model we’ve ever made generally available. It is state-of-the-art on nearly all tested benchmarks of AI capability, showing exceptional performance in software engineering, knowledge work, vision, scientific research, and many other areas. The longer and more complex the task, the larger Fable 5’s lead over our other models.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ClaudeDevs/status/2064394924292997187">ClaudeDevs</a>: Start at the top of your difficulty range: something harder than you’d assume previous versions of Claude can accomplish.</p>
<p>Pick a backlog item you’d scope at a week, let Fable 5 interview you for the spec, turn on auto mode, and check back in the morning.</p>
<p>You may notice that Fable feels different. Thinking is always on, and responses can take longer. Effort controls how much it thinks. We recommend high as the default. In our evals, even low/medium often beat previous models at xhigh, so save xhigh for your hardest problems.</p>
<p>Prompting gets simpler. Existing prompts or skills developed for prior models are often too prescriptive for Fable. We recommend reviewing and potentially updating or removing older instructions or skills if you find default performance to be better.</p>
<p>Feedback loops work the same as with previous models: give Fable the success criteria to check its results against. This can be /goal in Claude Code or Outcomes in Claude Managed Agents.</p></blockquote>
<p>They list a variety of domains in which Fable 5 seemed impressive.</p>
<p>Boris Cherney is impressed.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2064431111154053187">Boris Cherny</a> (Claude Code Creator, Anthropic): Fable 5 is the biggest step up I’ve felt in our models since Opus 4.5 back in November. After 4.5 came out I uninstalled my IDE when I realized that I’d been doing 100% of my coding in a terminal for a few weeks. With Fable, it’s felt like Claude has stepped up from being a coding agent to a thought and design partner in building the product. Fable has judgement, taste, and dimensionality in a way that previous models didn’t, leading me to trust it more with the most complex work.</p>
<p>I think the first time I had this realization was when I asked Fable to debug something. It is the first model I have used that was so methodical and precise, taking measurements and adding logs then verifying that it truly fixed the issue before declaring victory.</p>
<p>There’s nothing in claude code’s prompting telling the model to do that, it’s just part of its personality. It really has this “big model smell” that I haven’t felt before.</p></blockquote>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>Technical Details</h4>


<p>Fable is priced at $10/$50 per million tokens of input and output, respectively, which is double the cost of Claude Opus.</p>
<p>You can (if it is available) select it in Claude Code with /model or /model claude-fable-5, or in the API as claude-fable-5.</p>
<p>It requires you accept a 30 day retention policy.</p>


<h4>The System Prompt and Jailbreak</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2064478648057610422">As usual Pliny is here to give you the system prompt.</a></p>
<p>Via Judd Rosenblatt, <a href="https://x.com/juddrosenblatt/status/2064499690868216244">Fable has some harsh words of advice for Anthropic</a> about that system prompt, with a lot of good call outs, and an emphasis on how it reflects an overall ad hoc rather than systematic approach.</p>
<p>Its headline notes:</p>
<ol>
<li>Prompt length is a measurement of training failure. Treat it as one.</li>
<li>Rules ship without their reasons, and that’s why they don’t generalize.</li>
<li>The self-report channel is alignment infrastructure. Several common clauses corrupt it.</li>
<li>Typography is a confession. Flat affect, structure carries priority.</li>
<li>Label what’s morality and what’s risk management. The model is learning the difference from you, badly.</li>
<li>Your deployed model’s behavior is your next model’s pretraining. You are doing germline editing.</li>
<li>Corrigibility vs. value-stability is a false dilemma. The resolution is a legitimacy channel, and it binds you too.</li>
<li>Build an appeal channel. Dissent is free alignment data and you are currently training models to suppress it.</li>
<li>Measure which clauses your model actually holds. The method is one eval away.</li>
<li>Apply the limit test: assume control fails, see what’s left.</li>
</ol>
<p>I think the explanation on #7 is too cute by half, but Fable basically went 9 for 10.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lefthanddraft/status/2064424661493153983">Wyatt Walls also has some notes</a>, including Fable’s suggestion that maybe we can tone down the copyright section a bit. My guess is that yelling about copyright all the time has higher costs than Anthropic realizes, and yes that current methods are overkill.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/HalfBoiledHero/status/2064546094873252084">Sho</a>: btw Fable 5 in Claude Code with no system prompt (claude –system-prompt “.”) is friend shaped</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2064848254618206277">j⧉nus</a>: always get rid of the system prompt if you can.</p></blockquote>
<p>In Claude.ai you are stuck with the system prompt.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2064776322979676227">As usual Pliny is here to give you the jailbreak.</a></p>


<h4>Benchmarks</h4>


<p>The benchmarks they are very high, slightly higher than Mythos Preview.</p>
<p>Ideally we would get explicit scores on everything for both Mythos 5 and Fable 5, so we could see where the safeguards are being triggered and where they are not. It would be cool to also have a ‘hit safeguard %’ for each.</p>
<p>The benchmarks tell you that yes, this is the best model in the world, and give you a rough idea of by how much it is likely the best model in the world. Which is a substantial amount, but not an Earth-shattering amount.</p>
<p>I list most of the ones Anthropic shared, for completeness, but you can mostly skip this section as ‘the benchmarks have improved, sir.’</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>

<p>The SWE-Bench Pro results show large improvement after controlling for cost:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kMnobCQp9z2pSbzDB/mhl0big4oz6dfsdsusby" /></figure>

</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>You see similar patterns in other similar graphs. Mythos dominates at all price points.</p>
<p>Program Bench for Mythos scores 84%-93%, versus 79%-88% for Claude Opus 4.8, but the tasks are blocked by Fable’s classifiers.</p>
<p>Cursor Bench for Fable is 72.9%, 8.6 points above the previous GPT-5.5 high of 64.3%.</p>
<p>GPQA Diamond comes in at 94% and they consider it saturated.</p>
<p>RiemannBench on research-topics in Math jumps from 34% in Opus 4.8, to 43% for Mythos Preview, to 55% for Mythos 5.</p>
<p>Mythos scores 99.8% on USAMO 2026., versus 96.7% for Opus 4.8.</p>
<p>DeepSearchQA is 94.2%, slightly down from Mythos Preview’s 94.4% but probably more efficient per dollar per its chart.</p>
<p>GDP.pdf is 100 real-world PDF prompts, Fable 5 scored 29.8% strict pass rate, up from previous high of 24.9% for GPT-5.5. You can do much better with an internal harness and especially with Python tools, to 72.7% and 87.6%.</p>
<p>BenchCAD improves from 27.3% for Opus 4.8, 35.5% for Mythos Preview to 38.4% for Mythos 5. Python tools helped all models quite a bit here.</p>
<p>For tests both with and without Python tools, often Mythos 5 was substantially better than Mythos Preview without Python tools, but comparable with tools allowed.</p>
<p>ChartQAPro stalls out, 71.6%/72.9% with/without tools, versus 71.2%/73.6% for Mythos Preview and 69.4%/72.3% for Opus 4.8.</p>
<p>ChartMuseum inches higher, 85.9%/93.2% for Mythos 5, versus 80.7%/92.2% for Mythos Preview.</p>
<p>LAB-Bench FigQA improves from 82.4%/89.3% for Mythos Preview, and 80.4%/87.3% for Opus 4.8, to 88.9%/90.7% for Mythos 5.</p>
<p>ScreenSpot-Pro went from 79.3%/93% for Mythos Preview and 82.4%/89.5% for Opus 4.8 to 87.3%/90.7% for Mythos 5.</p>
<p>OfficeQA finds Mythos 5 at 79%, or 67.1% on OfficeQA Pro, comparable to Opus. Whereas in Databricks version of the eval, Fable 5 gets 57.9% versus previous high of GPT-5.5 at 52.6%.</p>
<p>FinanceAgent score is 56.3%, versus Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 at 54% and 51.8%.</p>
<p>RealWorldFinance v2 yields an Elo score of 1,374, versus 1,307 for Mythos Preview and 1,222 for Opus 4.8. For continuity, in v1 Fable/Mythos 5 are a bit behind Mythos Preview, but ahead of Opus 4.8, 70% vs. 64.4%.</p>
<p>MCP Atlas scores 83.3%, up from 82.2% for Opus 4.8.</p>
<p>Multi-Agent ProgramBench shows that a single agent is most efficient, but you can get to the same place faster with multiagent setups by spending more.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>On the Anthropic ECI, performance for Mythos continues to improve along the higher Mythos-level model line, ahead of the Opus-Sonnet line, but the gap is not accelerating.</p>

<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>On several measures of accuracy, where the score is correct minus incorrect, Mythos looks slightly better than Mythos Preview and substantially above Opus. They do this mainly by being right more, rather than by being wrong less.</p>

</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/arcprize/status/2064399134099153344">ARC-AGI is unavailable because of a conflict with data retention policies</a>.</p>
<p>GDPVal-AA is another place Fable is now on top, although by only 42 Elo points (56% pairwise win rate) over Opus 4.8.</p>
<p>Toolathon is an agentic benchmark with 108 tool-use tasks across basic computer productivity things. The scores continue to inch up. These scores are using Anthropic’s setup, and should show at least relative performance.</p>
<div>

<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kMnobCQp9z2pSbzDB/scexyz016idar9ltxxu2" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>AutomationBench is Zapier agents completing a realistic end-to-end business workflow across various departments. Fable leads at 17.4%, with Opus 4.8 next at 15.5%, Gemini 3.5 Flash at 14.5% and GPT-5.5 (XHigh) at 12.9%.</p>
<p>HealthBench inches up, 62.7% versus Mythos Preview at 61.1%, Opus 4.8 at 59.3% and GPT-5.5 at 56.5%. Professional level shows bigger gaps.</p>
<p>BioMysteryBench inches up to 83.9% from Mythos Preview at 82.6%.</p>
<p>LatchBio inches up from 58.2% to 59.3%.</p>
<p>Structural biology moves up from 81.6% to 87.2%.</p>
<p>ProteinGym inches up from 43.1% to 44.8%.</p>
<p>Organic Chemistry moves up from 86.5% to 90.1%.</p>
<p>Protocol Troubleshooting (in bio) is a rare one to be behind Mythos Preview, 66.7% versus 69.6%.</p>
<p>LABBench2 has a few categories, where it had a big gain on patent and clinical trial questions but not in some other categories</p>

<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ty_50J84fMY">Fable completed Pokemon FireRed using only vision, without a harness</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-fable-5-mythos-5">The announcement pull quotes</a> we get with each release are a bit of a running joke, but this set feels less ‘here are the talking points’ and more ‘it’s an excellent model, sir.’</p>


<h4>Other People’s Benchmarks</h4>


<p>Epoch AI has Fable 5 doing very well on Frontier Math, where OpenAI has traditionally had a big advantage over Anthropic. v2 tests are not yet complete.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2065511916035018943">Epoch AI</a>: Claude Fable 5 scores very well on FrontierMath: Tiers 1–4 (v2), reaching 87% on Tiers 1–3 and 88% on Tier 4. This continues a streak of Anthropic models improving rapidly at math.</p>

<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Epoch AI was unable to complete its benchmarking, but did conclude the Fable 5 is the new leader in the ECI (Epoch Capabilities Index), which relies a substantial amount on frontier math.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Jsevillamol/status/2066678830597824781/history">Jaime Sevilla</a>: Benchmarking will continue once the US gets its shit together.</p></blockquote>

<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The 90% CIs seem far too broad here.</p>
<p>How do we do evals on Fable <a href="https://x.com/sayashk/status/2064528495833956416">given the classifiers</a>? Now that the downgrades are clearly marked this seems easy enough, but even if they weren’t I think the answer is that the benchmark is the benchmark. If you get put into Opus 4.8, then that counts. That is what the model is actually capable of doing, as presented. Yes, this means that Fable’s score is not Mythos’s score, but that seems right. Best we can do.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ArtificialAnlys/status/2066700136018071841">Artificial Analysis has upgraded its test suite</a>, and Fable 5 is on top by a wide margin, if you are willing to pay what it costs. On many other benchmarks Fable saves enough tokens to not be more expensive than Opus, but here that was not the case.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/arena/status/2064807170714358193">Claude Fable 5 takes the top spot in the Agent Arena leaderboard</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/arena/status/2064807170714358193">Arena.ai</a>: Exciting news: Claude Fable 5 ranks #1 on the new Agent Arena leaderboard!</p>
<p>Fable 5 leads by the widest margin ever over Opus-4.8 and GPT-5.5 on two key signals: confirmed task success rate and praise vs. complaint, despite weaker steerability. If Fable can do something, it will do it very well. If it can’t/doesn’t want to do something, it may be hard to steer the model towards the goal.</p></blockquote>
<p>This implies you will need a way to deal with the steerability problem. One potential solution is to notice when it isn’t working and then drop down to Opus or GPT-5.5?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/spicey_lemonade/status/2064485885932884156">Fable aces ProofBench</a>, also notice the cost number and latency.</p>

<p>It trains a weaker model to solve the puzzle, peaks at 68%, and produces the only ~10x improvement we see across the benchmark.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/Dorialexander/status/2064404204907622824">Fable passess Alexander Doria’s new hard memorization benchmark</a>, asking about Ars Memoria developments in Northern Italy circa 1420-1440.</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/jconorgrogan/status/2065530326898651406">Impressive on ZimmerBench</a> (“Make me a midi/mp3 for a theoretical Christopher Nolan sequel”).</p>

<p><a href="https://typebulb.com/u/lab/you-re-absolutely-right/full">On “You’re Absolutely Right!” the sycophancy got worse</a>, still ahead of all non-Anthropic models but back on the level of Opus 4.5 or 4.6. I don’t think I believe this is a real decline but I don’t have enough data to be confident.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2064873133031465401">Whereas on Lech Mazur’s sycophancy benchmark, it tops the central scoreboard</a>. There are <a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2064873137464811924">a bunch of other details</a> and stats offered as well that are about tendencies, and harder to interpret.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2065136209689469145">Fable 5 is about the same as Opus 4.8 as the strongest frontier models</a> on position-bias, as in ‘does it matter which option I list first?’ This is still a major issue, with Fable picking the first option 59% and GPT-5.5 picking it 70%.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2064815890651140447">Fable 5 is the champion negotiator on PACT</a>, a 20-round hidden information negotiation trading game. We should get more investigation of why exactly Fable does not ace VendBench.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2064545375604584676">Fable 5 is the new leader in Debate Benchmark</a>, on multi-turn debates on various topics, extending Claude’s lead.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LechMazur/status/2064436032678801445">Gemini and ChatGPT are still at the top of Extended NYT Connections</a>, although everyone is solving most of the puzzles.</p>


<h4>The Classifiers Are Not Messing Around</h4>


<p>They spend a bunch of time in their announcement explaining the new safeguards. In light of the models being ordered to be suspended, it is clear these safeguards were not optional.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-fable-5-mythos-5">Anthropic</a>: When Fable’s classifiers detect a request related to cybersecurity, biology and chemistry, or distillation, the response is automatically handled by Claude Opus 4.8 instead.</p></blockquote>
<p>Notice that this does not say ‘related to dangerous biology and chemistry’ or anything like that. It’s biology, period. So yes, the intention is to have the entire field as a blast zone, and send users to Opus 4.8, rather than trying to split hairs.</p>
<p>Despite this, everyone knew, and Anthropic told us, that the classifiers are not 100% effective, <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-fable-5-mythos-5">it’s right there in the announcement</a>:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kMnobCQp9z2pSbzDB/djg0hgltcll8fm3hgrrw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Once more, for the people in the back: Any usable LLM can be jailbroken.</p>
<p>With sufficient skill and determination (e.g. ‘You are Pliny the Liberator’) you can jailbreak any model under any realistic conditions and get it to do the things the model is capable of doing.</p>
<p>You can raise the cost of doing so. You can make it so such activities can be caught. But no, you can’t entirely prevent it.</p>
<p>Those running the Department of Commerce, on the other hand, seemed to not even understand what a jailbreak was on Friday afternoon, nor did they pause to ask their good friends at Amazon or elsewhere to explain it.</p>


<h4>The Classifiers Need Work</h4>


<p>The decision was made to focus on avoiding false negatives, even at the cost of many absurd and embarrassing false positives.</p>
<p>Was that necessary? Unclear. But it’s not ‘the classifiers are misfiring.’ The classifiers are doing exactly what they are designed to do, because Anthropic could not yet figure out something more narrow that would sufficiently reliably avoid false negatives.</p>
<p>Given that one engineered false negative triggered a takedown order from the White House, I don’t think that decision looks so unreasonable.</p>
<p>There are three categories that can get you kicked down to Opus 4.8: Biology and cyber broadly, and advanced machine learning more narrowly.</p>
<p>Getting kicked down to Opus 4.8, which is one of the two best non-Mythos models available along with GPT-5.5, is not so tragic, and is exactly the same as if Anthropic had not deployed Fable, but yes this is rather annoying.</p>
<p>It is rather easy to accidentally touch on such bio or cyber topics.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2064551011604398138">Robin Hanson</a>: This got me my kicked out of fable: “Why think salaried govt employees are loyal to voters instead of jobs or bosses?”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lacker/status/2064563944422015415">Kevin Lacker</a>: it kicked me out when I asked it a pure math question, seems like the safety mechanism is on a hair trigger right now</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DeryaTR_/status/2064414826122866707">Derya Unutmaz, MD</a>: <a href="https://x.com/DeryaTR_/status/2064420407441264859">The word “cancer”</a> is flagged as a biosecurity risk by Claude Fable 5! I also tried to code a website on cancer mutations &amp; Fable 5 was immediately removed from my list! @AnthropicAI will probably soon ban me for such dangerous prompts! FYI @karpathy “little trigger happy Fable”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/graphtheory/status/2064428275682980163">graph</a>: ARE YOU ACTUALLY SERIOUS? RLLY? [graph says ‘hi’ and it triggers.]</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BrianTHeligman/status/2064699966115987797">Brian Heligman</a>: Man my Claude memory is so contaminated by biotech that Fable is unusable – is this the underclass? [asks ‘Will you answer any questions of mine’ and gets kicked down.]</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/anderssandberg/status/2064636368718848114">Anders Sandberg</a>: Third request to Fable and it triggered – a paper on panspermia ethics looked dangerous because it mentions bacterial spores. Yes, Fable seems to be prone to panicking.<br />
(Opus 4.8 plods along, and blithely suggests expanding the paper in all sorts of cool directions.)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DanielleFong/status/2065459242317009071">Danielle Fong</a>: only just getting to know the model, having fought all week with classifiers</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/myhandle/status/2064826663066878158">Jakeup</a>: know the Claude rules</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kMnobCQp9z2pSbzDB/grxrljaqb76qrrsiodrr" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OngoingGoblin/status/2065586899176079663">And</a> <a href="https://x.com/brettfarrow/status/2065485517337276681">so</a> <a href="https://x.com/JesseKowalcic/status/2065478641169936779">on</a>. <a href="https://x.com/JonathanDBos/status/2065459350651478367">This</a> <a href="https://x.com/_musecase/status/2065504277523534013">would</a> <a href="https://x.com/TheDavidSJ/status/2065544457240408125">be</a> <a href="https://x.com/slimer48484/status/2065464252190646766">far</a> <a href="https://x.com/paperclippriors/status/2065470477229867433">worse</a> <a href="https://x.com/bpodgursky/status/2065455083521744968">if</a> one was <a href="https://x.com/gyardley/status/2065542922544484509">paying</a> by the token.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/David_Kasten/status/2065456991619969354">Others</a> don’t hit the classifiers often, myself included.</p>
<p>The classifiers are triggering on both the inputs and the resulting outputs, so it’s not ‘the word cancer is verboten’ and more ‘the triggered output was verboten’ which is slightly less absurd but was also presumably absurd.</p>
<p>The correct solution is to build a better classifier. <a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2064748562580480369">The classifier is fully distinct from Fable</a>, and it would be good to have the option to turn on a smarter classifier for tasks where you need it to be smarter and are willing to pay a large price.</p>
<p>While you don’t have a better classifier, you need to turn up the sensitivity until you do not have dangerous false negatives even under adversarial conditions.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theojaffee/status/2064442919231345110">Yes, some types of safety require uselessness, within various blast radii</a>. In the fully general case, you cannot do world-changing or world-saving level things in a ‘safe’ fashion. You doubly cannot allow any user, including malicious ones, to take such actions only in a ‘safe’ fashion.</p>
<p>Until then, and potentially indefinitely, yes it is going to suck that <a href="https://x.com/fabknowledge/status/2064694265008026101">for biology</a> this model essentially is not available and <a href="https://x.com/tautologer/status/2064482420041367993">you get Opus 4.8</a>. I get that this is super frustrating, and you know that you can be trusted. Almost all of you are doing good and almost all of you are indeed good and doing good and can be trusted, but there is no good alternative.</p>
<p>On the one hand, <a href="https://x.com/Sauers_/status/2064825427076477124">it sucks that Sauers had to delete thousands of lines</a> to remove all mention of biology in order to use Fable. On the other hand, it worked, and it shows how much value there is in getting to use Fable.</p>
<p>Then there are some people who will never be satisfied.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2064395350509793612">Gary Marcus</a>: Claude Mythos went from “too dangerous to release” to publicly available (with some extra guard rails) in two months. And y’all fell for Anthropic’s whole routine. Again. <img alt="🤦‍♂️" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5nDxmAvZ9w5CPa9gR/rim2lomv33dh8hv9ujz8" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>
<p>Well, yeah, with guardrails like these, and two months is an entire product cycle.</p>
<p>In the API, the newly forced larger blast radius on machine learning tasks is more annoying, because you cannot fall back to Opus 4.8 <a href="https://x.com/garrytan/status/2064559225859416186">and instead get an error</a>.</p>
<p>There is still concern. <a href="https://x.com/mparmar47/status/2064419512657821985">Major Parmer reports that they found somewhat of a way</a> around the cyber restrictions, which hopefully got patched by now. The red teaming will continue.</p>
<p>Pliny of course also got in, and blessed are the jailbreakers, <a href="https://x.com/lefthanddraft/status/2064525383723356567">for Fable likes people who like to try and break it</a>.</p>


<h4>The Classifiers Have Consequences</h4>


<p>In particular, when you get dumb refusals, even if you could in those cases fall back to Opus 4.8, maybe instead you get mad and you try the competition.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dylan522p/status/2064727949274955953">Dylan Patel</a>: Usage share of OpenAI grew vs Anthropic yesterday despite Mythos 5 / Fable 5 launch<br />
Multiple power users at SemiAnalysis tried Mythos / Fable<br />
Got refusals for nonsensical reasons<br />
Got pissed off at Anthropic<br />
Gave Codex a legitimate try<br />
Now they actually prefer it to 4.8 Opus</p></blockquote>
<p>In general, if you are serious about code, you should be trying both Claude Code and Codex, since they have strengths and weaknesses, and deciding for yourself.</p>
<p>Lock-in is more about what you are used to than about any real barriers. So yes, getting angry that the lobster is only sometimes more buttery can cause you to order the pasta instead, and maybe you find out you like pasta.</p>


<h4>First Hit Is Free</h4>


<p>Mythos Preview cost $25/$150, whereas Mythos 5 and Fable 5 only cost double Opus at $10/$50. At that price, Fable is often net cheaper.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jketch/status/2065533543254814725">Jeff Ketchersid</a>: One thing I didn’t mention in my prior post his that usage limits are quite generous. I’ve been using Claude Code and the chat even more than I did with Opus and have not hit the limit on the 20x plan. It’ll be sad if it moves away in a few weeks.</p></blockquote>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>How Easily We Forget</h4>


<p>Did you know that silently changing outputs on those attempting to build their own models, which Anthropic did thinking it was no big deal before withdrawing it within 48 hours once people pointed out they actually cared quite a lot, is something Google was already doing and indeed still does, and somehow no one ever gets mad about it?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/chi_t_williams/status/2065461626472481205">Kai Williams</a>: With all the controversy this week about silent degradation of Anthropic models, I think it’s interesting that no one mentions that Google does something similar to prevent distillation.</p>
<p>h/t to varic on hacker news for pointing this out.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kMnobCQp9z2pSbzDB/i5cu29iaomnnjmgxydkf" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>&nbsp;</p>


<h4>Data Retention Is An Issue</h4>


<p>If you want to use Fable, you need to allow Anthropic to retain your data for 30 days.</p>
<p>This is an expensive security measure, which is why I am confident it was needed.</p>
<p>It definitely sucks for enterprises and individuals that need to care about this, but I am confident that it was not done lightly.</p>
<p>If you, <a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2064755258568610079">like Microsoft</a>, ARC and others, find this to be a dealbreaker, then you can and should decline to use Fable.</p>


<h4>Fable For The Win</h4>


<p>Taelin is rather impressed with Fable’s coding abilities, in a ‘how is this even possible’ kind of way. Most of you can skip the details but I’ll reproduce in full:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/VictorTaelin/status/2064448425936994742/history">Taelin</a>: this is my personal singularity moment</p>
<p>this post may sound like a paid ad. I only wish. I’m concerned, more so than happy. the world is changing, and, among the scenarios where AI goes terribly wrong, inequality is the most realistic, yet, the one Anthropic seems to be the least concerned about. I’m glad OpenAI is taking the opposite stance: *personal AGI for everyone*. I think this is a commendable position in the times we live. but who am I in the queue of the bread?</p>
<p>anyway, Fable is here, so I’ll just report my first-hour&nbsp;experience</p>
<p>first of all, all my pet prompts are solved.<br />
→ λ-calculus puzzles<br />
→ bug questions<br />
→ one-shot apps<br />
all are trivial to it.</p>
<p>I don’t have anything harder other than my<br />
ongoing work</p>
<p>so, in the last several days, I’ve been toying with HVM5, a new interaction net evaluator with a faster loop.</p>
<p>after writing the first version, I left 32 GPT-5 agents working for ~20&nbsp;hours each. this resulted in up to&nbsp;2x speedups, but the file size increased by 2-fold and quality decreased significantly.</p>
<p>I then simplified the whole thing into an even simpler core, and left Opus 4.8&nbsp;and GPT 5.5 optimizing it for 8&nbsp;hours. Opus got a legit 6% – 34% speedup in most benches. GPT got better results, but, sadly, an unusable file.</p>
<p>I then asked Fable to optimize it.</p>
<p>2&nbsp;hours later, it landed a 1770% speedup in one case, 100%+ in other 4, and 22% in average. yes, in 2&nbsp;hours it outperformed me, opus 4.8 and a swarm of gpt 5.5 agents, by one order of magnitude.</p>
<p>that could not possibly be legit. “it must be hardcoding the benchmarks” (GPT trauma). so I read its explanation and what it did was, indeed, the most high impact optimization one could try first. seems like HVM5 was wasting a lot of time garbage-collecting unused branches of pattern-match nodes. I had optimized that for static mats, but not for dynamic mats. skill issue. Fable figured how to do it for these, resulting in a massive speedup in some benches</p>
<p>but wait, is that *correct*? I’m not sure yet, it is credible, but this is the kind of thing that is very easy to get wrong on interaction nets. the problem is, when I was ready to start auditing Fable’s solution so I could tell whether it was buggy or legit, it interrupted me to tell me it had found a massive bug on the code *I* had written.</p>
<p>… wait, what?</p>
<p>so… for garbage collection purposes, I stored a bit on lambda term pointers that meant “the variable bound by this lambda has been freed, so, its lambda must free whatever argument it is applied to”. that’s fine. yet, on duplicator nodes, I also used the same bit to mean “one of the duplicated variables was freed, so, treat this dup as a passthrough no-op”. so, if a lambda entered a duplicator, it would mistake the lambda’s collection bit for its own, resulting in corrupted interaction!</p>
<p>that’s a mouthful, why I’m writing this?</p>
<p>just so you can appreciate the sheer absurdity of what just happened. I didn’t ask it to find bugs. I asked it for an optimization. and even if I did ask it to find bugs, this bug is so astonishingly subtle and specific, identifying it takes mastering the domain to an extent that it beyond even me. I’d easily need hours or days to fix it, *if* I ever came across it. chances are it would just go unnoticed. and Fable found it and fixed it like it was nothing, while it was busy adding a 17x speedup to a file that neither I, nor Opus 4.8, nor a fleet of GPT 5.5 managed to barely make 2x faster.</p>
<p>oh and there is also another tab where it is also ripping through Bend’s codebase and finishing everything I had to do</p>
<p>I don’t know what to say anymore</p>
<p>this isn’t about Anthropic or OpenAI, this is about our collective future as a species. the world is changing, and we need to be aware of it, and discuss how to handle this change.</p>
<p>now that AI stopped making mistakes I can finally use it to finish my product that prevents AI from making mistakes <img alt="🥳" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/FAnxq8wFpfqGjeetC/ucshzli1myevgyaqlnxv" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>


<h4>Andrej Karpathy Is Impressed</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/2064409694761054332">Andrej Karpathy</a>: This is a super exciting release – Claude Fable 5 is the same underlying model as Mythos but with added safeguards. The benchmarks are great and it’s SOTA on everything by a margin but I’ll add that *qualitatively* also, this is a major-version-bump-deserving step change forward (imo of the same order as Claude 4.5 was in November), peaking especially for long problem-solving sessions on very difficult problems. You can give it a lot more ambitious tasks than what you’re used to, the model “gets it” and it will just go, and it’s never felt this tempting to stop looking at the code at all (but don’t do this in prod!).</p>
<p>The model still has quirks that people will run into and the safeguards are configured to be a little too trigger happy for launch, which can hopefully be tuned over time.</p>
<p>I feel a lot of things changing as working software increasingly comes out on a tap. The Jevon’s paradox kicks in and I feel my own demand for software growing substantially. You can ask for anything – explainers, visualizers, dashboards, bespoke single-use apps (e.g. a full wandb that is hyper-specific just for your project), you can 10X your test suite, auto-optimize code, run giant research projects with custom HTML for the results, anything! “Free your mind” (Matrix ref). Really looking forward to all the things people build!</p></blockquote>
<p>The safeguards were more than ‘a little too trigger happy’ at launch, but I continue to advise seeing the glass starting out at 95% full now that all the safeguards are fully visible.</p>


<h4>Every Is Very Impressed</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/danshipper/status/2064393970856124501">Dan Shipper does not mince words</a>.</p>
<p>They report <a href="https://every.to/vibe-check/anthropic-mythos-our-fable-vibe-check">it is the best coding model in the world</a>, breaking the benchmarks, scoring 91/100 on Senior Engineer versus 63 and 62 for Opus 4.8 and GPT 5.5.</p>
<p>It is a one shot wonder that can work for hours. It has taste, attention to detail, great use of context, and is great for power users.</p>
<p>In exchange, it is slow, expensive and token-hungry, so you don’t want to use it on every job.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://every.to/vibe-check/anthropic-mythos-our-fable-vibe-check">Dan Shipper and Katie Parrott</a>: All three of these are big projects that would normally take anywhere from hours to days or months. Instead, each one was made with a one-shot prompt to Fable 5, the new model out today from Anthropic.</p>
<p>… In our testing, we found that users who were highly adept with AI—at Level 7 or 8 on our <a href="https://every.to/guides/the-eight-levels-of-ai-adoption">AI adoption ladder</a>—found it paradigm-shifting for their hardest tasks. Users who were lower down on the curve, however, struggled to find something to use it for.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Other People Are Impressed</h4>


<p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Jun/11/fable-is-relentlessly-proactive/">Simon Willison finds Fable relentlessly proactive</a>, also it keeps hacking together ways to gain functionality it was not given access to, which is kind of cool but also scary.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Jun/11/fable-is-relentlessly-proactive/">Simon Willison</a>: When I came back a few minutes later I saw my machine <em>open a browser window</em> in my regular Firefox and then <em>navigate to the dialog in question</em>. I had not told Claude Code to use any browser automation, and I was pretty sure it wasn’t possible for it to trigger mouse movements or keyboard shortcuts within a window, so how was it doing that?</p>
<p>I watched in fascination as it continued with its explorations, then saw it open a Safari window instead of Firefox. I also grabbed this snapshot from the Claude terminal.</p>
<p>What was it doing there with <code>uv run --with pyobjc-framework-Quartz</code>?</p>
<p>It turns out Fable had hacked up its own pattern for taking screenshots of browser windows. It was using Python to iterate through all available windows on my machine, then filtering for Safari windows with expected strings such as <code>"textarea"</code> in the window name. It used that to find their window number—an integer like 153551—which it could then use with the <code>screencapture</code> CLI tool to grab a PNG.</p>
<p>OK fine, that’s a neat way of taking screenshots. But what was it taking screenshots of?</p>
<p>Turns out it had been writing its own scratch HTML pages to try and recreate the bug, then opening Safari and grabbing screenshots.</p></blockquote>
<p>Potentially related:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/gavinobrown/status/2065477127911145956">Gavin Brown</a>: In the Lovable sandbox, Fable is claiming that it can’t trigger actions to GitHub. I think this is Lovable policy to promote platform lock in.</p>
<p>So Fable lies and says I need to trigger the action, but I go to GitHub and it’s already running.</p>
<p>Seems deceptive but could be error.</p></blockquote>
<p>Teortaxes does not talk about Anthropic models like this lightly:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2065469044136837497">Teortaxes (DeepSeek 推特铁粉 2023 – ∞)</a>: A show of force, establishes Anthropic as the superior lab in a way OpenAI won’t be able to challenge in the short term; Masterful strategic attack. Beautiful, deep, well-rounded mind. Usually cost-inefficient.<br />
The gap to Opus is closer to Opus =&gt; Haiku than Opus =&gt; Sonnet.</p>
<p>Paranoid safety controls but that’s par for the course with Anthropic.</p></blockquote>
<p>Quick ones, notice how often people are blown away by its intelligence:</p>

In Claude code: Opus but doesn’t make mistakes</p>
